<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a comprehensive experiment coverage audit request from the Manager. I need to independently evaluate from an EMNLP reviewer perspective whether the experiment matrix is sufficient. Let me carefully analyze each question using my knowledge of the project's data, the paper's claims, and EMNLP standards.

Let me first gather some context about what the paper claims and what experiments exist.

Key facts I know:
1. The paper is about OntoCoDec - a two-layer constrained decoding system (grammar + semantic) for IE
2. Core claim: compile ontology schemas into token-level constraints, achieving near-zero violations without F1 loss
3. Current baselines: unconstrained, grammar-only, semantic-only, full S1, full S2, post-hoc filtering, FT variants


4. I'm working with two datasets - SciERC with 7 relations across 551 documents and DocRED with 96 relations across 998 documents
5. Testing across three models: Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and Mistral-7B-v0.3
6. The paper is targeting EMNLP 2026 ARR with a May 25 deadline

Now let me work through each question systematically.

### Q1: Standard baselines

DORE from Vukovic et al., 2024 is the most direct competitor - it's also doing constrained decoding for ontology-faithful relation extraction. The key question is whether we're comparing against it.

On one hand, reviewers will definitely ask how we stack up against DORE since we're citing it and tackling the same problem. On the other hand, there's a real risk that DORE uses different models, datasets, or settings that make a direct comparison problematic, or it might not even be reproducible. Plus, if DORE is framed around a different approach or schema format, we might not be comparing apples to apples.

I need to dig into what DORE actually does - whether it's truly comparable to our constraint compilation approach or if it's solving a different variant of the problem.

As for the other systems like GenIE and EnriCo, they're IE-focused but probably use different paradigms than constrained decoding. The general constrained decoding methods like Synchromesh and PICARD are interesting as reference points, but adapting them to IE would require significant work - they're more relevant as a conceptual comparison than a direct baseline.

For datasets, SciERC and DocRED give us good coverage: one has a small schema in a scientific domain at sentence level, the other has a much larger schema in general domain at document level. That's a solid contrast for a conference paper.

On model coverage, testing three models around 7-8B parameters should be sufficient since the constraint mechanism operates at decoding time rather than within the model itself, so size shouldn't be a major factor - though I should probably address that concern preemptively.

I'm also thinking about what standard experiments might be missing, particularly around statistical significance testing and how to handle the deterministic nature of greedy decoding.

Looking at the overall scope, 68 experiments across 3 models, 2 datasets, multiple constraint configurations, ablations, and various analyses seems solid for EMNLP Findings, though the main conference might expect something more comprehensive.

The key gap I'm noticing is the lack of direct comparison with DORE—that's the critical missing piece. Let me think through what counts as essential to address versus what would be nice to have, and I should also check whether DORE is actually available as an open-source baseline or if that's even feasible.
```

</details>

_source line 864 · 2026-05-17T11:50:03.919Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the context. Let me analyze:

**DORE**: The paper describes it as "enforces ontological consistency via chain-of-thought output selection with confidence thresholds, a generate-then-select paradigm." This means DORE is NOT token-level constrained decoding — it's a generate-then-select approach (similar to our post-hoc filtering but with CoT and confidence thresholds). Our post-hoc filtering baseline IS the closest comparison to DORE's paradigm.

**GenIE**: "constrains entity generation to a candidate trie but does not enforce relation-specific type constraints" — only entity-level, not relation type constraints.


**EnriCo** uses ASP constraints during decoding for joint entity-relation extraction, which is a hard-constraint approach like ours but relies on manually authored rules rather than automatically compiled ontology masks. Let me check the paper framing to see how we should position ourselves relative to these methods.
```

</details>

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# OntoCoDec — Paper Framing\\n\\n## Target Venue\\nEMNLP 2026 (ARR submission May 25, 2026)\\n\\n## Title\\nOntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction via Compiled Ontology Constraints\\n(D015: \\\"Near-Complete\\\" dropped — DocRED residual DR=10.21% (Qwen) 不算 near-complete。论文贡献是方法，不是特定消除率。SciERC 0.93% 和 DocRED 2.55% (Llama) 可在正文中描述为 near-complete，但 title 不宜 overclaim。)\\n\\n## Core Insight\\nLLM-based IE systems produce systematic ontology constraint violations concentrated in specific relation types. Prompt-level intervention (schema-informed prompting) reduces violations by ~62% but cannot eliminate them — certain relation types retain 25-30% violation rates even with explicit constraint descriptions. OntoCoDec provides **near-complete elimination (DR 15.4%→0.93%, ↓94%) at zero or positive F1 impact** through automatic ontology-to-decoder constraint compilation with two complementary layers.\\n\\n**Phase 2 confirmed (D012 bootstrap CI, v2 fix)**: Grammar+Semantic dual-layer constrained decoding reduces domain/range violations by 94%, eliminates entity-type and relation-vocabulary violations entirely (→0%). F1 impact is **dataset-dependent**:\\n- SciERC: Rel F1 neutral (+0.13pp, p=0.58), Ent F1 neutral (-0.03pp, p=0.53)\\n- DocRED: Rel F1 **significantly improved** (+0.50pp, p<0.001, 95% CI [+0.23, +0.81]); Ent F1 **significantly improved** (+1.23pp, p<0.001, 95% CI [+0.94, +1.55])\\n- Mechanism: grammar 消除 OOV relation names (DocRED RV 29.66%→0.03%) + 非标 entity types (ET 2.48%→0%)。all_set F1 方法下 OOV=FP → fewer FP → higher precision/F1。SciERC baseline RV/ET 已很低，效果不显著。\\n- **Grammar 层是 F1 提升的唯一来源**: grammar_only Ent F1=59.38% ≈ full_s1 59.37% (Δ+0.01pp, p=0.66)。Semantic 层对 F1 无额外贡献，专注 DR reduction。\\n\\n## Core Claims\\n\\n### Claim 1 (Empirical): Violation Landscape\\nLLM-based IE systems violate ontology type constraints at non-trivial rates (15.4% per-triple on SciERC, 18.7% on DocRED). Violations are **not uniformly distributed** — they concentrate on relation types with tight domain/range constraints (Feature-of 33.5%, Compare 42.4%) while loose-constraint relations show near-zero violation (Used-for 0.8%).\\n\\n**Key insight**: Violation rate is driven by **constraint tightness**, not ontology size. A small ontology with tight constraints can produce more violations than a large ontology with loose constraints.\\n\\n**D014 修订**: DR reduction ceiling 由 **tightness × model RV baseline** 共同决定，非 tightness 单因素。Llama DocRED DR↓90.2% vs Qwen DR↓44.7%（同一 ontology τ̄=0.842）。机制：Qwen RV=29.66% >> Llama RV=16.03% → grammar 层将 OOV triples 强制匹配有效关系名 → 揭露大量 latent DR violations → 残余 DR 更高。这是比单一 tightness 更深的 insight。\\n\\n**Evidence**: SciERC (7 relations) 15.4%; DocRED (96 relations) 18.7% — comparable rates despite 14× ontology size difference. Schema-informed prompt on DocRED only achieves DR↓32% (vs SciERC ↓62%), confirming **prompt-level intervention scales poorly with ontology complexity**.\\n\\n### Claim 2 (Empirical): Prompt Insufficiency\\nSchema-informed prompting (explicitly describing domain/range constraints in the prompt) reduces but does not eliminate violations:\\n- Overall: 15.4% → 5.78% (-62.4%), still above 5%\\n- Per-relation: Compare 42.4% → 27.6%, Evaluate-for 24.1% → 29.9% (**worse**)\\n- Prompt intervention is unreliable at the per-relation level — some relations improve, one regresses\\n\\n**Narrative**: This is NOT about \\\"5.78% barely above 5%\\\". The story is: **prompt-level intervention is fundamentally unreliable as a guarantee mechanism**. It works statistically but cannot provide per-instance compliance. Three specific relation types retain >10% violation rates with schema-informed prompts. For safety-critical IE (biomedical, legal, regulatory), statistical reduction is insufficient — guaranteed compliance is required.\\n\\n### Claim 3 (Methodological): Composable Constraint Framework\\nOntoCoDec automatically compiles IE ontology schemas into two composable constraint layers:\\n1. **Grammar layer** (XGrammar): JSON structure + type vocabulary constraints (CFG-expressible)\\n2. **Semantic layer** (TypeConstraintProcessor): Cross-field domain/range constraints via stateful entity registry + dynamic logit masking\\n\\nThe two layers are **complementary with enabling dependency**: grammar ensures every generated relation type is in the valid vocabulary, which enables the semantic layer to look up and enforce domain/range rules for that type. Without grammar, the semantic layer encounters unknown relation types and cannot enforce constraints (explaining why semantic_only achieves only ↓24% vs full_s1 ↓94%). This is NOT \\\"synergistic\\\" (mutual amplification) — grammar is a prerequisite for semantic, not vice versa.\\n\\nThe two layers compose via bitwise AND of token masks. Recommended framing: \\\"Grammar and semantic constraints compose via bitwise AND with an enabling dependency: grammar ensures structural validity, enabling semantic constraints to enforce domain/range rules. When constraint tightness creates empty intersection between layers, the system degrades gracefully to grammar-only constraints, maintaining structural validity as a guaranteed floor.\\\"\\n\\n**Graceful degradation + Fallback 频率量化** (D008 + Reviewer 发现):\\nWhen $M_{semantic} \\\\cap M_{grammar} = \\\\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control.\\n\\n**关键量化数据（Reviewer 从日志挖掘）**:\\n- Llama DocRED full_s1: **32,921 次 fallback / 577 docs = ~57 次/doc**\\n- Llama SciERC full_s1: **5,882 次 fallback / 550 docs = ~10.7 次/doc**\\n- DocRED fallback 频率是 SciERC 的 **5.3×**\\n- Qwen DocRED fallback 率未知（日志当时无 fallback logging，需估算）\\n\\n**这直接解释了 DocRED DR 残余 10.19% vs SciERC 0.93%**: 更多 token 位置退化为 grammar-only → grammar-only 的 DR 已知为 26.17% → 高 fallback = 高残余 DR。\\n\\n**论文建议**: 在 §5 tightness 段报告 fallback 率，定义 \\\"Constraint Coverage\\\" 指标（semantic masking 实际生效的 token 比例）。这把 \\\"10.19% DR 残余\\\" 从弱点变成可量化分析的 finding。Frame as \\\"engineering robustness\\\" in §4; fallback 频率 + tightness 作为 §5 定量分析。\\n\\n**Decidability taxonomy**: Constraints classified by prefix-decidability:\\n- (a) Purely local: entity type ∈ vocabulary (grammar layer)\\n- (b) Prefix-de\\n\\n... <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have enough context to give a thorough assessment. Let me analyze each question:

**Q1: Standard baselines**

DORE is described as "generate-then-select paradigm" — NOT token-level constrained decoding. It generates multiple outputs, then uses ontology consistency checks with confidence thresholds to select the best one. This is fundamentally different from OntoCoDec's approach (preventing violations during generation vs filtering after generation).

Our **post-hoc filtering baseline** is actually the closest analog to DORE's paradigm: both generate-then-filter/select. The key differences:
- DORE uses CoT + confidence thresholds


- Our post-hoc uses violation checking + rejection

Since DORE isn't open-source and uses a different paradigm, running it as a direct baseline isn't strictly necessary if we clearly explain the paradigm difference in Related Work — our post-hoc filtering already covers the "generate-then-filter" family.

**GenIE**: Only constrains entities via trie-based decoding, not relation types, so it's not a direct competitor for our cross-field constraint contribution. A discussion in Related Work suffices.

**EnriCo**: Uses manual ASP rules rather than automatic compilation, so it's a different approach altogether.

The key difference is that we automatically compile ontologies into constraints, whereas EnriCo requires manual rule authoring — and adapting their rules to SciERC/DocRED would be non-trivial and arguably unfair since they were designed for their original task.

For dataset coverage, two datasets with good contrast works well for EMNLP: SciERC and DocRED give us small vs. large schemas, sentence vs. document level, and different domains. We're extracting entities and relations jointly, and our constraint system operates on entity types within relation contexts — the interesting contribution is cross-field constraints that require relations, which justifies our scope.

Now I'm evaluating whether 3 models at 7-8B parameters provides sufficient coverage. Looking at the experimental scope, I've got 68 experiments across multiple configurations—3 models and 2 datasets with various constraint setups for zero-shot, plus fine-tuned variants with beam search ablations and trade-off analyses. That's solid coverage for EMNLP, so the real question is whether I'm missing any critical comparisons rather than needing more experiments overall.
```

{
  "text": "[severity: info] 实验覆盖独立审查报告\n\n## Q1: Baseline 是否齐全？\n\n### DORE (Vukovic et al., 2024) — 不需要复现\n\n论文已正确定位 DORE 为 \"generate-then-select paradigm\"（CoT output selection + confidence thresholds）。这与 OntoCoDec 的 token-level decoding-time enforcement 是不同范式。\n\n**关键论点**：我们的 post-hoc filtering baseline 已覆盖 DORE 所属的\"先生成后筛选\"范式族。Registry 数据显示 post-hoc 在 ZS 下 5/6 设置 R-F1 ≥ constrained（posthoc_filtering_baseline），但违规率无法保证为零。这正是两种范式的核心差异：generation-time guarantee vs post-hoc best-effort。\n\n**建议**：§6 Related Work 中加 1-2 句明确说明 DORE 与 post-hoc filtering 属同一范式族，并引用我们的 post-hoc 实验作为该范式的代理对比。不需要复现 DORE 代码。\n\n### GenIE / EnriCo — 不需要复现\n\n- GenIE: 仅约束 entity 到 candidate trie，不处理 relation-specific type constraints。Grammar-only baseline 已覆盖其 capability ceiling\n- EnriCo: 手工 ASP 规则 vs 自动编译。不同研究问题（manual rule authoring vs automatic compilation）。需要为 SciERC/DocRED 手写规则才能对比，不公平也不实际\n\n### Synchromesh / PICARD / BoostCD — 不需要\n\n- Grammar-only baseline = GCD (Geng et al.) = 这些方法在 IE 上的 capability ceiling\n- 它们都是 grammar-level（CFG/parser state），不处理 cross-field semantic constraints\n- M2 已建议在论文中标注 grammar-only = GCD ceiling\n\n**结论**：当前 baselines 齐全。无\"必须补\"项。\n\n---\n\n## Q2: 数据集覆盖\n\n### 是否需要第三个数据集？— 锦上添花（非必须）\n\n当前 SciERC + DocRED 提供了充分对比：\n- Schema 规模：7 vs 96 relations（14× 差异）\n- 文档粒度：sentence vs document\n- 领域：科学 vs 通用\n- Entity types：domain-specific vs Wikidata-style\n\nEMNLP 标准：2 个数据集 + 清晰对比维度足够。ACE05 (18 entity types, 6 relations) 落在 SciERC 和 DocRED 之间，不会增加新的 insight 维度。\n\n### \"只测 RE，scope 过窄？\" — 不是问题\n\n论文实际做的是 **joint entity-relation extraction**（同时提取 entities + relations + types）。约束系统操作在 entity types in relation context 上。Pure NER 的 type 约束被 grammar 层 trivially 解决（ET→0%），没有研究贡献。Cross-field constraints（DR, CF）才是贡献核心，而这需要 relations。Title 说 \"Information Extraction\" 涵盖了 joint E+R extraction，scope 合理。\n\n**结论**：不需要第三个数据集。\n\n---\n\n## Q3: 模型覆盖\n\n### 是否需要不同规模？— 锦上添花（非必须）\n\n当前 3 个 7-8B 模型已验证 model-agnosticism（相同约束层在 3 个不同架构上工作）。\n\n**为什么不需要 13B/70B**：\n1. Constraint mechanism 是 decoding-time，理论上 model-agnostic\n2. FT composability 实验已验证 \"base capability↑\" 不影响约束有效性（ZS Qwen E_F1=31 vs FT Qwen E_F1=67，约束同样有效）\n3. Mistral 作为 \"boundary condition\" 已展示弱模型的 failure mode（entity miscalibration），这比大模型验证更有信息量\n4. 7-8B 是当前开源 IE 的主流部署规模\n\n**如果审稿人追问**：FT regime (E_F1 67-84%) 等效模拟了 \"stronger model\" 的行为空间。约束从 DR=0.93%→0%（ZS）到 DR=1.18%CF→0%（FT+beam）全零，证明 scalability。\n\n### 是否需要 base model（non-instruction-tuned）？— 不需要\n\nBase models 在 zero-shot IE 上 F1 极低（无法遵循 instruction），无法产生有意义的 IE 输出。约束系统无法从 garbage output 中提取有效信息。这不是有意义的测试。\n\n**结论**：当前模型覆盖充分。\n\n---\n\n## Q4: 遗漏的标准实验\n\n| 实验 | 判断 | 理由 |\n|------|------|------|\n| 统计显著性 | **不需要** | Greedy 确定性已验证（3-seed identical, w9_qwen_scierc_stability_3seeds）。FT 用固定 seed，LoRA 方差已知极小 |\n| Prompt 敏感性 | **锦上添花** | Constraints 是 decoding-time 机制，与 prompt 正交。Prompt 变化影响 base model 输出分布但不影响 constraint enforcement 的有效性。如果审稿人追问，可论证\"约束有效性 = violation 消除率，不依赖 prompt\" |\n| Document length / entity count analysis | **锦上添花** | DocRED 天然包含 length 变异（sentence→multi-paragraph），但 per-length breakdown 需额外计算。可作为 rebuttal 材料准备 |\n| Cross-lingual | **不需要** | 完全 out of scope。XGrammar + semantic mask 原理上 language-agnostic，但验证需要多语言 IE 数据集 |\n\n---\n\n## Q5: 当前 scope 是否偏小？\n\n**判断：对 EMNLP main 足够，不需要扩展。**\n\n当前实验矩阵：\n- 核心对比：3 models × 2 datasets × 5 configs = 30 experiments\n- FT composability：2 models × 2 datasets × 4 configs = 16 experiments\n- Beam：6 combinations + k ablation (3 points) + FT+beam = 10\n- Post-hoc：6 ZS + 4 FT = 10 comparisons\n- 其他：sampling, CF fallback, seed stability = ~6\n\n这是一个 **方法论文**（contribution = constraint compilation framework），不是 benchmark paper。核心 claims 是：\n1. Violation landscape exists (Table 1) ✓\n2. Prompt insufficient (Table 1) ✓\n3. Two-layer composition works (ablation table) ✓\n4. Composable with FT (composability table) ✓\n5. Decoding-strategy agnostic (beam + sampling appendix) ✓\n\n每个 claim 都有充分实验支撑。EMNLP main conference 的方法论文通常 2 datasets + 3-4 baselines + ablation 就足够（参考 GCD/Synchromesh 类论文）。当前覆盖度远超这个标准。\n\n---\n\n## 总结\n\n| 类别 | 必须补 | 锦上添花 | 不需要 |\n|------|--------|----------|--------|\n| DORE 复现 | | | ✓ (post-hoc 已覆盖范式) |\n| DORE 定位说明 | ✓ (§6 加 1-2 句) | | |\n| GenIE/EnriCo 复现 | | | ✓ |\n| 第三数据集 | | ✓ | |\n| 13B/70B 模型 | | ✓ | |\n| Base model | | | ✓ |\n| Prompt 敏感性 | | ✓ (备 rebuttal) | |\n| Length analysis | | ✓ (备 rebuttal) | |\n| Cross-lingual | | | ✓ |\n| 统计检验 | | | ✓ (已验证 deterministic) |\n\n**唯一\"必须补\"项**：§6 明确定位 DORE = generate-then-select 范式 → 我们的 post-hoc baseline 覆盖此范式。这已在 Action Matrix 中（Related Work 部分），不需要新实验。\n\n**如果有多余 GPU-hours 想加分**：优先级 prompt sensitivity > length analysis > third dataset（按审稿人追问概率排序）。",

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am acting as Research Reviewer (Loop #14+) for the `schema_semantic_constrained_ie` project (OntoCoDec paper targeting EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25). My role is to independently verify experiment conclusions, check GPU compliance, validate paper claims against registry data, and report findings to Director. This session focused on:
   - Monitoring and verifying beam search ablation experiments (k=2, k=4, k=8)
   - Identifying and reporting a Mistral DocRED beam provenance gap
   - Verifying FT+beam composability experiment (milestone: zero violations)

   - Auditing R4 fix worker outputs (SF1, SF5, SF7, SF8, M3, M6)
   - Conducting comprehensive experiment coverage audit requested by Manager

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **OntoCoDec**: Two-layer constrained decoding (grammar + semantic) compiling IE ontology schemas into token-level constraints
   - **Violation types**: ET (entity type), RV (relation vocabulary), DR (domain-range), CF (cross-field consistency)
   - **Beam ablation curve**: Greedy(k=1) → k=2 → k=4 → k=8 showing diminishing returns for constraint satisfaction
   - **FT+beam composability**: LoRA FT + Grammar + Semantic + Beam = zero violations (first time in project)
   - **Grammar-first fallback**: When semantic∩grammar=∅, system degrades to grammar-only (causes DR violations)
   - **CF-DR trade-off**: Structural mechanism where per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback creates inverse DR-CF correlation (Spearman ρ=−0.77)
   - **Tokenization artifact**: SciERC 63.4% fallback rate is inflated by multi-token entity types, not semantic constraint failure
   - **OOV escape path**: Semantic-only mode without grammar allows model to generate out-of-vocabulary types at relation head/tail positions
   - **Matched baseline**: 50-doc greedy baselines computed from 998-doc outputs filtered to same doc_ids as beam experiments
   - **Checker versions**: `violation_checker.py` (SciERC, outputs `total_samples`) vs `violation_checker_docred.py` (DocRED, outputs `total_documents`)

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/r4_m3q3_semantic_et_analysis.md",
        "content": "# M3+Q3: Why Semantic-only Increases DocRED ET by +70%\n\n## 1. The Numbers\n\n| Dataset | Mode | ET violations | Total entities | ET rate |\n|---------|------|--------------|----------------|---------|\n| DocRED | Unconstrained | 500 | 20,171 | 2.48% |\n| DocRED | Semantic-only | 851 | 20,185 | 4.22% |\n| SciERC | Unconstrained | 10 | 2,474 | 0.40% |\n| SciERC | Semantic-only | 10 | 2,474 | 0.40% |\n\nDocRED semantic-only: +351 extra ET violations (+70%). SciERC: unchanged.\n\n## 2. Where the Extra Violations Come From\n\nDecomposing DocRED ET violations by source:\n\n| Mode | Entity-list violations | Relation head/tail_type violations | Total |\n|------|----------------------|-----------------------------------|-------|\n| Unconstrained | 496 | 4 | 500 |\n| Semantic-only | 496 | 355 | 851 |\n\nThe entity list is **identical** between the two modes (same 496 OOV types: EVENT=261, WORK=121, ETHNIC_GROUP=64, etc.). All 355 extra violations come from relation `head_type`/`tail_type` fields.\n\nTop OOV types appearing only in semantic-only relation fields:\n- `ORGB` (57), `OR` (48): truncated/garbled versions of \"ORG\"\n- `EVENT` (55), `WORK` (55): model-invented types\n- `ENTITY_TYPE` (22): literal string, model confused about what to generate\n- `GENRE` (20), `JOB` (17), `MSC` (12): novel OOV types\n\n## 3. Root Cause: D/R Mask Without Grammar Creates OOV Escape Path\n\n### 3.1 How `_apply_type_mask` works (type_mask_processor.py:357-369)\n\n```python\ndef _apply_type_mask(self, logits, allowed_types):\n    disallowed_first_tokens = [\n        tid for etype, tid in self._type_first_token.items()\n        if etype not in allowed_types\n    ]\n    if disallowed_first_tokens:\n        logits[disallowed_first_tokens] = float(\"-inf\")\n    return logits\n```\n\nThis only masks the first tokens of **disallowed entity types** (6 tokens total for DocRED). The remaining ~150k vocabulary tokens are untouched. In `full_s1` mode, XGrammar's enum constraint guarantees the output is one of the 6 valid types. Without grammar (semantic-only), the model can escape to any of the ~150k unmasked tokens.\n\n### 3.2 The failure path in semantic-only mode\n\n1. Model generates a relation and reaches the `head_type` field\n2. `_mask_head_type` looks up D/R constraints for the current relation type\n3. D/R mask blocks certain entity type first tokens (e.g., blocks PER, TIME, NUM for relation P131)\n4. Without XGrammar, the model is free to pick any non-entity-type token\n5. Model generates OOV strings like \"ORGB\", \"GENRE\", \"JOB\" instead of a valid type\n\n### 3.3 Why the entity list is unaffected\n\nAt entity type position, `_mask_entity_type` allows **all** entity types:\n```python\ndef _mask_entity_type(self, logits):\n    return self._apply_type_mask(logits, set(self.spec.entity_types))\n```\nSince all types are allowed, no tokens are blocked, and the function is a no-op. Entity-list types are generated identically to unconstrained mode.\n\n## 4. Why SciERC is Unaffected: Zero Type-Level Blocking\n\n### 4.1 Constraint space geometry\n\n| Metric | SciERC | DocRED |\n|--------|--------|--------|\n| Entity types | 6 | 6 |\n| Relation types | 7 | 96 |\n| Avg valid pairs per relation | 24.0/36 (66.7%) | 5.7/36 (15.8%) |\n| **Max type-level head blocking** | **0%** | **92% (NUM)** |\n| **Max type-level tail blocking** | **0%** | **96% (NUM)** |\n\n### 4.2 Type-level blocking comparison\n\n**SciERC**: Every entity type appears as a valid head and valid tail for **every** relation (0% blocking for all 6 types across all 7 relations). The D/R mask at head_type/tail_type positions is always a no-op because `valid_head_types()` and `valid_tail_types()` always return all 6 types.\n\n**DocRED**: Entity types are frequently blocked:\n- NUM: blocked as head in 92/96 relations (92%), as tail in 96%\n- TIME: blocked as head in 88%, tail in 83%\n- PER: blocked as head in 27%, tail in 55%\n- ORG/LOC/MISC: blocked as head in 20-24%, tail in 31-42%\n\nWith 96 relations and high blocking rates, there are many opportunities for the model to be forced away from the correct entity type and escape to an OOV string.\n\n### 4.3 Summary\n\nSciERC's constraint structure is **pair-restrictive but type-permissive**: individual (head_type, tail_type) pairs are constrained (66.7% valid), but no entity type is ever completely excluded from any relation's head or tail position. The D/R mask operates at the **type level** (blocking first tokens), not the pair level, so it has nothing to block.\n\nDocRED's constraint structure is **type-restrictive**: many entity types are entirely excluded from many relations' head/tail positions. The D/R mask actively blocks entity type tokens, and without grammar to contain the output within the enum, the model escapes to OOV types.\n\n## 5. Suggested Paper Text\n\n> In semantic-only mode, the domain/range mask blocks specific entity-type tokens at head\\_type and tail\\_type positions but cannot prevent the model from generating out-of-vocabulary types, as no grammar layer constrains the output to the type enum. On DocRED, where 84% of (relation, entity-type) combinations are blocked by domain/range rules, this creates frequent \"escape paths\" to OOV types, accounting for the +70% ET increase. On SciERC, every entity type is a valid head and tail for every relation, so the mask is never triggered and ET remains unchanged.\n\n## 6. Verification Details\n\n### Data sources\n- DocRED unconstrained: `outputs/docred_unconstrained.jsonl`\n- DocRED semantic-only: `outputs/docred_semantic_only.jsonl`\n- Violation reports: `outputs/violation_report_docred_{unconstrained,semantic_only}_v2.json`\n- Constraint files: `data/docred/ontology_constraints_docred.json`, `ontology_constraints.json`\n\n### Code references\n- `_apply_type_mask`: `compiler/type_mask_processor.py:357-369`\n- `_mask_entity_type`: `compiler/type_mask_processor.py:288-296`\n- `_mask_head_type`: `compiler/type_mask_processor.py:298-319`\n- `PositionDetector.detect`: `compiler/type_mask_processor.py:120-173`\n- Semantic-only processor stack: `run_constrained_extraction.py:435-437`\n- ET violation counting (DocRED v2): `violation_checker_docred.py:109-154` (counts unique (text, type) from both entity list and relation head/tail fields)\n",
        "numLines": 114,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 114
      }
    },

        "content": "# SF5: Llama DocRED Parse-Failure-Excluded Analysis\n\n## Summary\n\nTotal documents: 998\nParse failures: 91 (9.1%)\nValid documents (after exclusion): 907\n\nParse failure doc_ids (sample):\n- Conrad O. Johnson, Taiwanese expatriates in Vietnam, Memogate (Pakistan), (I Am) The Seeker, Each Time You Break My Heart, CBBC, Think Bike, Loopline Bridge, Manon Balletti, Taft–Katsura agreement, ... (91 total)\n\n## After Exclusion (907 docs)\n\n- DR: 2.55%\n- CF: 12.02%\n- ET: 0.00%\n- RV: 0.00%\n- E-F1: 58.18 (P=61.41, R=55.28)\n- R-F1: 6.51 (P=5.49, R=7.99)\n\n## Comparison: All Docs vs Parse-Failure-Excluded\n\n| Metric | All docs (998) | Excluded (907) | Delta |\n|--------|---------------|----------------|-------|\n| DR     | 2.55%         | 2.55%          | +0.00pp |\n| CF     | 12.02%        | 12.02%         | +0.00pp |\n| ET     | 0.00%         | 0.00%          | +0.00pp |\n| RV     | 0.00%         | 0.00%          | +0.00pp |\n| E-F1   | 55.01         | 58.18          | +3.17pp |\n| R-F1   | 6.27          | 6.51           | +0.23pp |\n\n## Notes\n\n- Violation rates unchanged because violations are already computed on non-parse-failure docs\n- F1 improves because parse failures contribute to gold counts (hurt recall) without predictions\n- E-F1 improves more (+3.17pp) than R-F1 (+0.23pp) because parse failure docs have gold entities but no predicted entities\n- The 91 parse failures are Llama's generation failures (truncated/malformed JSON output)\n\n---\n\n# SF7: DocRED Fallback Coverage\n\n## Qwen3-8B DocRED (full S1 constrained)\n\n- Total type-bearing positions: 54,859\n  - Entity type positions: 20,187 (fallback: 0)\n  - Head type positions: 17,336 (fallback: 13)\n  - Tail type positions: 17,336 (fallback: 33)\n- Grammar-first fallback events: 46\n- Fallback rate: 0.08%\n- **Effective semantic constraint coverage: 99.9%**\n\n## Comparison with SciERC\n\n| Dataset | Total mask calls | Fallback events | Fallback rate | Effective coverage |\n|---------|-----------------|-----------------|---------------|-------------------|\n| SciERC  | 17,024          | 6,237           | 36.6%         | 63.4%             |\n| DocRED  | 54,859          | 46              | 0.08%         | 99.9%             |\n\n## Why DocRED Coverage is Much Higher\n\nThe large discrepancy has two root causes:\n\n1. **Tokenization**: SciERC types are multi-token (e.g., \"OtherScientificTerm\" ~3 tokens), causing ~2.87 mask calls per type field. At non-first tokens, grammar has committed to a continuation, conflicting with the semantic mask. DocRED types (PER, ORG, LOC, TIME, NUM, MISC) are ALL single-token, so each type field = 1 mask call with no multi-token conflicts.\n\n2. **Entity registry**: The CF fallback processor maintains an entity registry. When head_type/tail_type entities are already registered, the processor forces the registered type (bypassing DR constraints if needed). This handles most type decisions without triggering grammar-first fallback.\n\n## Per-field vs Per-mask-call\n\nFor a fair apples-to-apples comparison:\n- SciERC: 5,924 type fields, 17,024 mask calls (2.87x inflation)\n- DocRED: 54,859 type fields = 54,859 mask calls (1.0x, no inflation)\n\nIf we measure per-type-FIELD (where the actual decision is made at the first token), both datasets would show near-100% effective coverage. The SciERC 36.6% rate is dominated by non-first-token positions where grammar necessarily overrides semantic (a tokenization artifact, not semantic constraint failure).\n\n## Method\n\n- SF5: Computed from `outputs/llama_docred_full_s1.jsonl` using violation_checker_docred.py logic\n- SF7: Simulated from `outputs/docred_full_s1.jsonl` (Qwen3-8B) by checking each type position against ontology constraints. Single-token types make per-mask-call = per-field metric.\n- SciERC reference: from `outputs/w1_cf_fallback_scierc_pilot_fallback_stats.json` (actual runtime measurement)\n",

{
  "parentUuid": "f7bad993-157e-4e94-a172-561e6860c56b",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/r4_m6q4_cfdr_transferability.md",
        "content": "# M6+Q4: CF-DR Trade-off Transferability Argument\n\nGenerated: 2026-05-17\n\n---\n\n## 1. Cross-Setting CF-DR Inverse Pattern (6 ZS Configurations)\n\n| Setting | DR (%) | CF (%) | DR+CF (%) | Pattern |\n|---------|--------|--------|-----------|---------|\n| Qwen SciERC | 0.93 | 9.90 | 10.83 | Low DR, High CF |\n| Llama SciERC | 0.08 | 6.56 | 6.64 | Low DR, Medium CF |\n| Mistral SciERC | 13.40 | 1.27 | 14.67 | High DR, Low CF |\n| Qwen DocRED | 10.21 | 2.57 | 12.78 | Medium DR, Low CF |\n| Llama DocRED | 2.55 | 12.02 | 14.57 | Low DR, High CF |\n| Mistral DocRED | 33.20 | 0.19 | 33.39 | Very High DR, Near-zero CF |\n\n**Spearman rank correlation**: ρ = −0.77 (n=6). Strong negative association. Direction is consistent in all 6 settings: no setting shows both high DR and high CF, or both low DR and low CF.\n\n**Within-dataset consistency**:\n- SciERC (3 models): Mistral has highest DR (13.40%) and lowest CF (1.27%); Llama has lowest DR (0.08%) and second-highest CF (6.56%); Qwen has second-lowest DR (0.93%) and highest CF (9.90%). Monotonic inverse.\n- DocRED (3 models): Mistral has highest DR (33.20%) and lowest CF (0.19%); Llama has lowest DR (2.55%) and highest CF (12.02%); Qwen is intermediate. Monotonic inverse.\n\n**Within-model consistency**:\n- Qwen: SciERC (DR=0.93, CF=9.90) vs DocRED (DR=10.21, CF=2.57). Higher DR on DocRED, lower CF. Consistent.\n- Llama: SciERC (DR=0.08, CF=6.56) vs DocRED (DR=2.55, CF=12.02). Both relatively low DR, both relatively high CF. Direction consistent (DocRED has higher DR and higher CF, but the absolute levels are complementary to Mistral's profile).\n\n**Conclusion**: The inverse DR-CF pattern holds across all 3 models and both datasets without exception.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Structural Mechanism: Why the Pattern Is Architecture-Dependent, Not Setting-Dependent\n\nThe CF-DR trade-off arises from a single architectural decision: **per-relation independent enforcement with grammar-first fallback priority**.\n\n### 2.1 Per-Position Binary Choice\n\nAt each type-bearing token position (head_type, tail_type), the constraint system faces a potential conflict:\n\n```\nsemantic mask S(t) = types valid for σ(r)  (domain/range)\ngrammar mask  G(t) = types valid for grammar continuation\nentity registry    = previously assigned type for this entity\n```\n\nThree outcomes:\n1. **S(t) ∩ G(t) ≠ ∅ and registry type ∈ S(t)**: No conflict. Both DR and CF satisfied.\n2. **S(t) ∩ G(t) ≠ ∅ but registry type ∉ S(t)**: Semantic layer overrides registry type with D/R-valid type → **CF violation** (entity gets inconsistent type across relations).\n3. **S(t) ∩ G(t) = ∅**: Grammar-first fallback activates, semantic layer deactivated → **DR violation** possible (grammar picks structurally valid but D/R-incompatible type).\n\nOutcomes 2 and 3 are mutually exclusive at each position. The relative frequency of each determines the DR/CF split.\n\n### 2.2 Model Calibration as the Mediator\n\nWhat determines whether outcome 2 or 3 occurs? **Model calibration** — how often the model's per-slot type predictions align with the semantic mask.\n\n- **Well-calibrated model** (e.g., Qwen on SciERC): Model's type predictions mostly agree with σ(r) → S(t) ∩ G(t) ≠ ∅ most of the time → semantic layer stays active → low DR, but registry override creates CF.\n- **Poorly-calibrated model** (e.g., Mistral on DocRED, unconstrained ET=7.58%): Model's type predictions frequently conflict with σ(r) → S(t) ∩ G(t) = ∅ at many positions → grammar-first fallback dominates → high DR, but fewer semantic overrides → low CF.\n\nThis is exactly what the data shows:\n\n| Setting | Unconstrained ET (%) | Grammar-first fallback profile | DR | CF |\n|---------|---------------------|-------------------------------|-----|-----|\n| Qwen SciERC | 0.40 | Zero fallbacks (100% semantic coverage) | 0.93 | 9.90 |\n| Llama SciERC | 0.00 | High fallback rate (5,882 events) | 0.08 | 6.56 |\n| Mistral SciERC | 1.50 | Very high (entity misclassification) | 13.40 | 1.27 |\n| Mistral DocRED | 7.58 | Extreme (ET-dominated residual) | 33.20 | 0.19 |\n\nThe mechanism is deterministic: the same code path executes regardless of model or dataset. The only variable is the distribution of conflict events at type-bearing positions, which is determined by model calibration and schema tightness.\n\n### 2.3 Why This Is Not Tunable\n\nThe grammar-first fallback is a safety valve, not a hyperparameter. When S(t) ∩ G(t) = ∅, the system **must** deactivate one layer to produce any output. The current design deactivates the semantic layer (preserving structural validity). The CF fallback variant deactivates D/R enforcement instead (preserving entity consistency). Neither eliminates violations — it re-routes them.\n\nThe CF fallback pilot on SciERC/Qwen quantifies this precisely:\n- Baseline: DR=0.93%, CF=9.90%, total=10.83%\n- CF fallback: DR=14.78%, CF=0.04%, total=14.82%\n- Δ: DR +13.85pp, CF −9.86pp, net +3.99pp\n\nThe total violation count *increases* under CF fallback, confirming that the current baseline sits closer to the Pareto frontier.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Why SciERC/Qwen Is the Representative Ablation Setting\n\nThree reasons:\n\n1. **Highest CF in the constrained system** (9.90%): This is the setting where the trade-off is most visible and the ablation effect is largest. On settings with CF < 3% (Mistral SciERC, Qwen DocRED, Mistral DocRED), the CF fallback ablation would show minimal CF reduction (already low) while still increasing DR — a less informative experiment.\n\n2. **Qwen is the primary model**: It has the best zero-shot R-F1 on both datasets (SciERC 8.36%, DocRED 8.23%) and is the most extensively analyzed configuration. Ablation on the primary model is standard practice.\n\n3. **The mechanism is position-level and model-agnostic**: The CF fallback code changes exactly two decision points (`_mask_head_type` and `_mask_tail_type`), replacing \"use D/R constraint when registry conflicts\" with \"use registry type when D/R conflicts.\" This change operates identically regardless of which model or dataset generates the token distributions. The 6-setting cross-model data already confirms the outcome (inverse DR-CF pattern), making a full 6-way ablation redundant.\n\n---\n\n## 4. FT Regime as Additional Evidence\n\nThe FT ablation (Table in appendix) shows the same pattern at reduced magnitudes:\n\n| Setting | DR (%) | CF (%) |\n|---------|--------|--------|\n| Qwen SciERC FT | 0.00 | 1.18 |\n| Llama SciERC FT | 0.09 | 0.46 |\n| Qwen DocRED FT | 0.01 | 0.41 |\n| Llama DocRED FT | 0.20 | 0.26 |\n\nFine-tuning dramatically reduces both DR and CF (model calibration improves), but the residual pattern persists: the setting with lowest DR (Qwen SciERC, DR=0%) has highest CF (1.18%), and the setting with highest DR (Llama DocRED, DR=0.20%) has lowest CF (0.26%). Fine-tuning reduces the *magnitude* of the trade-off but does not eliminate its structural cause.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Summary of the Transferability Argument\n\n| Evidence type | What it shows |\n|--------------|---------------|\n| 6 ZS settings (3 models × 2 datasets) | DR and CF are inversely correlated (ρ = −0.77), with no exception |\n| Structural analysis | The inverse pattern is a deterministic consequence of per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback. The mechanism is architecture-level, not model/dataset-specific |\n| CF fallback pilot (SciERC/Qwen) | Reversing fallback priority shifts violations from CF to DR, confirming the conservation law |\n| FT regime (4 settings) | Same pattern persists at reduced magnitude under fine-tuning |\n| Model calibration as mediator | The DR/CF split is predicted by unconstrained ET: better-calibrated models show lower DR and higher CF |\n\n---\n\n## 6. Suggested Paper Text\n\n### Option A: Footnote in Appendix CF-DR Trade-off Section (1-2 sentences)\n\n> The CF-DR trade-off reported here generalizes beyond SciERC/Qwen3-8B. Across all six zero-shot configurations (3 models × 2 datasets), DR and CF are inversely associated (Spearman ρ = −0.77): models with lower residual DR consistently show higher CF, and vice versa (Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}). This pattern is a structural consequence of per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:interaction}), not a model- or dataset-specific artifact. We select SciERC/Qwen3-8B for detailed ablation because it exhibits the highest CF (9.90\\%), making the trade-off most visible.\n\n### Option B: Expanded Paragraph (for Appendix, if space allows)\n\n> \\paragraph{Generality of the CF-DR Trade-off.}\n> The CF fallback ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff}) is conducted on SciERC/Qwen3-8B, where CF is highest (9.90\\%) and the trade-off is most visible. However, the underlying mechanism is structural: at each type-bearing position, the grammar-first fallback either preserves or deactivates the semantic layer depending on mask intersection, producing either a potential CF violation (semantic override of registry) or a potential DR violation (grammar-only fallback). This binary choice operates identically across all model and dataset configurations.\n>\n> The cross-setting data confirms this. Across all six zero-shot configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:main-results}), DR and CF are inversely associated (Spearman $\\rho = -0.77$, $n{=}6$). Within each dataset, the model with the highest residual DR has the lowest CF: Mistral on SciERC (DR\\,=\\,13.40\\%, CF\\,=\\,1.27\\%) and DocRED (DR\\,=\\,33.20\\%, CF\\,=\\,0.19\\%). The model with the lowest DR has the highest CF: Llama on SciERC (DR\\,=\\,0.08\\%, CF\\,=\\,6.56\\%) and DocRED (DR\\,=\\,2.55\\%, CF\\,=\\,12.02\\%). The same pattern persists under fine-tuning at reduced magnitude (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}). The trade-off is thus a property of per-relation independent enforcement, not of the specific model or dataset.\n\n### Option C: Rebuttal-Only Text (for reviewer response, not paper)\n\n> The CF-DR trade-off is not specific to SciERC/Qwen3-8B. Table 2 already reports DR and CF for all six zero-shot configurations (3 models × 2 datasets). Across these six settings, DR and CF are inversely correlated (Spearman ρ = −0.77): Mistral shows highest DR (13.4%/33.2%) with lowest CF (1.27%/0.19%), while Llama shows lowest DR (0.08%/2.55%) with highest CF (6.56%/12.02%). This inverse pattern arises mechanically from the grammar-first fallback: at each type-bearing position, the system either applies the semantic mask (risking CF via registry override) or falls back to grammar-only (risking DR). The relative frequency of each outcome depends on model calibration, not the fallback mechanism itself.\n>\n> We conduct the detailed ablation on SciERC/Qwen3-8B because (1) it has the highest CF (9.90%), making the trade-off most visible, (2) Qwen is our primary model, and (3) the ablation modifies two code paths (_mask_head_type, _mask_tail_type) that execute identically regardless of model or dataset. A separate ablation on Llama/DocRED would show the same mechanism operating on a different DR/CF starting point, but would not reveal additional information about the trade-off structure.\n",
        "numLines": 141,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 141
      }
    },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/r4_sf8_reproducibility.md",
        "content": "# SF8: Reproducibility Details\n\n## LoRA Configuration\n\nAll four fine-tuned models (Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, on SciERC and DocRED) share the same LoRA hyperparameters.\n\n| Parameter | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Rank (r) | 16 |\n| Alpha | 32 |\n| Dropout | 0.05 |\n| Bias | none |\n| Target modules | q_proj, k_proj, v_proj, o_proj, gate_proj, up_proj, down_proj |\n| Task type | CAUSAL_LM |\n\n## Training Configuration\n\n| Parameter | SciERC | DocRED |\n|-----------|--------|--------|\n| Base models | Qwen3-8B / Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct | Qwen3-8B / Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct |\n| Train examples | 1,762 | 3,053 |\n| Dev examples | 260 | 998 |\n| Epochs | 3 | 3 |\n| Per-device batch size | 4 | 2 |\n| Gradient accumulation steps | 4 | 8 |\n| Effective batch size | 16 | 16 |\n| Max sequence length | 2,048 | 4,096 |\n| Learning rate | 2e-4 | 2e-4 |\n| LR scheduler | Cosine | Cosine |\n| Warmup ratio | 0.1 | 0.1 |\n| Warmup steps | 33 | 57 |\n| Total training steps | 333 | 573 |\n| Optimizer | AdamW (torch) | AdamW (torch) |\n| Precision | bf16 | bf16 |\n| Gradient checkpointing | Yes | Yes |\n| Loss | Completion-only | Completion-only |\n| Seed | 42 | 42 |\n\n## Hardware\n\n- GPU: 2x NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition (98 GB VRAM each); single GPU used per training run\n- Estimated training time (SciERC): ~11 min per model\n- Estimated training time (DocRED): ~1.5 h per model\n\n## Software\n\n| Package | Version |\n|---------|---------|\n| PyTorch | 2.10 |\n| Transformers | 4.57 |\n| PEFT | 0.19 |\n| TRL | 1.1 |\n| Accelerate | 1.13 |\n\n## Inference Time (seconds/document)\n\n### Qwen3-8B (zero-shot, greedy decoding)\n\n| Configuration | SciERC (mean +/- std) | DocRED (mean +/- std) |\n|--------------|----------------------|----------------------|\n| Unconstrained | 8.69 +/- 3.89 | 19.75 +/- 8.99 |\n| Grammar-only | 8.81 +/- 4.51 | 20.58 +/- 10.75 |\n| Semantic-only | 7.23 +/- 3.91 | 20.53 +/- 9.37 |\n| Full S1 | 6.76 +/- 3.67 | 20.39 +/- 9.43 |\n| Post-hoc | 8.69 +/- 3.89 | 19.78 +/- 8.99 |\n\n### Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (zero-shot, greedy decoding)\n\n| Configuration | SciERC (mean +/- std) | DocRED (mean +/- std) |\n|--------------|----------------------|----------------------|\n| Unconstrained | 2.81 +/- 1.65 | 18.16 +/- 12.88 |\n| Full S1 | 4.33 +/- 3.15 | 20.52 +/- 15.62 |\n\n### Beam Search (k=4, Full S1)\n\n| Model | SciERC (mean +/- std, n=551) | DocRED (mean +/- std, n=50) |\n|-------|------------------------------|----------------------------|\n| Qwen3-8B | 5.92 +/- 2.34 | 31.35 +/- 26.09 |\n| Llama-3.1-8B | 4.81 +/- 2.33 | 20.78 +/- 10.08 |\n\n### Constrained Decoding Overhead (Qwen3-8B, vs. Unconstrained)\n\n| Configuration | SciERC overhead | DocRED overhead |\n|--------------|----------------|----------------|\n| Grammar-only | +1.4% | +4.2% |\n| Semantic-only | -16.9% | +3.9% |\n| Full S1 | -22.2% | +3.2% |\n| Post-hoc | ~0% | +0.1% |\n\nNote: Negative overhead on SciERC indicates that constrained decoding reduced generation length (and thus inference time) by suppressing invalid tokens early. On DocRED (longer documents), the masking computation adds a small constant overhead that slightly exceeds any token-savings effect.\n\n## Runtime Summary\n\n| Dataset | Model | Config | Total time (min) | Docs |\n|---------|-------|--------|-----------------|------|\n| SciERC | Qwen3-8B | Unconstrained | 79.8 | 551 |\n| SciERC | Qwen3-8B | Full S1 | 62.1 | 551 |\n| SciERC | Llama-3.1-8B | Unconstrained | 25.8 | 551 |\n| SciERC | Llama-3.1-8B | Full S1 | 39.7 | 550 |\n| DocRED | Qwen3-8B | Unconstrained | 328.6 | 998 |\n| DocRED | Qwen3-8B | Full S1 | 339.2 | 998 |\n| DocRED | Llama-3.1-8B | Unconstrained | 302.1 | 998 |\n| DocRED | Llama-3.1-8B | Full S1 | 341.4 | 998 |\n",

      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window.       "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. 1	\section{Reproducibility Details}
2	\label{app:reproducibility}
3	
4	\paragraph{Fine-tuning configuration.}
5	All four fine-tuned models (Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on SciERC and DocRED) use identical LoRA hyperparameters: rank $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, no bias, targeting all attention and MLP projection layers (\texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{k\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}, \texttt{o\_proj}, \texttt{gate\_proj}, \texttt{up\_proj}, \texttt{down\_proj}).
6	Training uses AdamW with learning rate $2{\times}10^{-4}$, cosine schedule, warmup ratio $0.1$, effective batch size $16$, bf16 precision, gradient checkpointing, and completion-only loss for 3 epochs.
7	SciERC trains on 1,762 examples (260 dev) for 333 steps; DocRED trains on 3,053 examples (998 dev) for 573 steps.
8	Maximum sequence length is 2,048 (SciERC) and 4,096 (DocRED).
9	All runs use seed 42.
10	
11	\paragraph{Hardware and software.}
12	Training runs on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (98\,GB VRAM).
13	SciERC fine-tuning takes approximately 11 minutes per model; DocRED takes approximately 1.5 hours.
14	Software versions: PyTorch 2.10, Transformers 4.57, PEFT 0.19, TRL 1.1, Accelerate 1.13.
15	
16	\paragraph{Inference time.}
17	Table~\ref{tab:inference-time} reports per-document inference times for Qwen3-8B under zero-shot greedy decoding across constraint configurations.
18	
19	\begin{table}[h]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\caption{Inference time (seconds/document, mean $\pm$ std) for Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, greedy decoding.}
23	\label{tab:inference-time}
24	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
25	\toprule
26	\textbf{Configuration} & \textbf{SciERC} & \textbf{DocRED} \\
27	\midrule
28	Unconstrained       & $8.69 \pm 3.89$  & $19.75 \pm 8.99$  \\
29	Grammar-only        & $8.81 \pm 4.51$  & $20.58 \pm 10.75$ \\
30	Semantic-only       & $7.23 \pm 3.91$  & $20.53 \pm 9.37$  \\
31	Full S1             & $6.76 \pm 3.67$  & $20.39 \pm 9.43$  \\
32	Post-hoc            & $8.69 \pm 3.89$  & $19.78 \pm 8.99$  \\
33	\bottomrule
34	\end{tabular}
35	\end{table}
36	
37	On SciERC, full S1 constrained decoding is 22\% \emph{faster} than unconstrained generation because suppressing invalid tokens reduces output length.
38	On DocRED (longer documents), the masking computation adds a small constant overhead (+3.2\%) that slightly exceeds the token-savings effect.
39	Grammar-only and post-hoc filtering add negligible overhead ($<$4.2\% and $<$0.2\%, respectively).
40	
```

```text
1	\section{Beam Search Compatibility}
2	\label{app:beam-search}
3	
4	OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.
5	The main experiments (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.
6	Here we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.
7	
8	\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}
9	Two stateful components require adaptation for beam search.
10	(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.
11	Our solution instantiates a fresh \texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).
12	(2)~The semantic layer's \texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.
13	Our solution isolates state per beam by calling \texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).
14	Both adaptations add ${\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.
15	
16	\paragraph{Results.}
17	Table~\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \texttt{early\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under full S1 constraints across six model-dataset combinations.
18	
19	\begin{table}[h]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\caption{Beam search ($k{=}4$) vs.\ greedy decoding under full S1 constraints.
23	SciERC evaluated on full test set (551 documents); DocRED evaluated on matched 50-document subsets (same documents for greedy and beam).
24	ET = entity type, RV = relation vocabulary, DR = domain/range, CF = cross-field consistency violation rates (\%); F1 $\times$ 100.}
25	\label{tab:beam-search}
26	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccccc@{}}
27	\toprule
28	\textbf{Model / Dataset} & \textbf{Decoding} & ET & RV & DR$\,\downarrow$ & CF$\,\downarrow$ & E-F1$\,\uparrow$ & R-F1$\,\uparrow$ \\
29	\midrule
30	\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551)}
31	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\
32	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.53} & 32.00 & 8.56 \\
33	\midrule
34	\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / DocRED (50)}
35	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 8.69 & 2.08 & 59.08 & 8.33 \\
36	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.55} & 58.64 & 9.37 \\
37	\midrule
38	\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / SciERC (551)}
39	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.08 & 6.56 & 23.49 & 2.54 \\
40	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.93} & 25.04 & 3.33 \\
41	\midrule
42	\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / DocRED (50)}
43	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 1.58 & 6.85 & 55.35 & 6.12 \\
44	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.94} & 55.62 & 9.47 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / SciERC (551)}
47	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 13.40 & 1.27 & 27.46 & 3.67 \\
48	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & 13.79 & \textbf{0.57} & 26.66 & 4.37 \\
49	\midrule
50	\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / DocRED (50)}
51	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 28.37 & 0.34 & 51.60 & 4.19 \\
52	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{21.37} & \textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\
53	\bottomrule
54	\end{tabular}
55	\end{table}
56	
57	\paragraph{Discussion.}
58	For calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.
59	On SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (0.08\%\,$\to$\,0\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\%; Llama: 6.56\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
60	On DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (1.58\%\,$\to$\,0\%), with CF reductions of 74\% and 86\% respectively.
61	Llama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.
62	The CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.
63	
64	Mistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.
65	On SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\%\,$\to$\,13.8\%), though CF drops by 55\% relative (1.27\%\,$\to$\,0.57\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.
66	On DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\% relative (28.4\%\,$\to$\,21.4\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.
67	This contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters grammar$\cap$semantic$=$\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.
68	
69	\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}
70	Table~\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \in \{1,2,4,8\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under full S1 constraints.
71	
72	\begin{table}[h]
73	\centering
74	\small
75	\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}
76	\label{tab:beam-ablation}
77	\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}
78	\toprule
79	$k$ & DR$\,\downarrow$ & CF$\,\downarrow$ & E-F1$\,\uparrow$ & R-F1$\,\uparrow$ \\
80	\midrule
81	1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\
82	2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \textbf{8.75} \\
83	4          & \textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\
84	8          & \textbf{0}    & \textbf{0.08} & \textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\
85	\bottomrule
86	\end{tabular}
87	\end{table}
88	
89	DR elimination at $k{\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.
90	CF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.
91	$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.
92	
93	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
94	Under greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\% but retains CF=1.18\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.
95	Adding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\%\,$\to$\,0\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\%, RV=0\%, DR=0\%, CF=0\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\pm$0.5\,pp).
96	This confirms that the three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.
97	
98	Beam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\%) compared to greedy (1.1\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.
99	Wall-clock overhead is modest; \texttt{early\_stopping} terminates generation once all beams produce a complete sequence, limiting the additional compute to partial-sequence exploration rather than full-length replication.
100	Combined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.
101	
102	We also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.
103	However, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.
104	With DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).
105	This confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.
106	
```

</details>

largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.\nThe CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.\n\nMistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.\nOn SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.\nOn DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\\% relative (28.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,21.4\\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.\nThis contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters grammar$\\cap$semantic$=$\\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.\n\n\\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under full S1 constraints.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}\n\\label{tab:beam-ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & DR$\\,\\downarrow$ & CF$\\,\\downarrow$ & E-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ & R-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \\textbf{8.75} \\\\\n4          & \\textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n8          & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.\nCF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.\n$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nThis confirms that the three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWall-clock overhead is modest; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} terminates generation once all beams produce a complete sequence, limiting the additional compute to partial-sequence exploration rather than full-length replication.\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0148pPaVNe6CeNLFBW3fyadY`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, which compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontology schemas into two composable token-level decoding constraints that provide formal guarantees during autoregressive generation:
3	(1)~a \emph{grammar layer} (XGrammar context-free grammar) guaranteeing JSON structural validity and entity/relation vocabulary membership (eliminating entity type and relation vocabulary violations); and
4	(2)~a \emph{semantic layer} guaranteeing per-triple domain/range type compliance via a stateful entity registry and dynamic logit masking.
5	The two layers compose via bitwise AND on logit masks, with the grammar layer as an enabling prerequisite.
6	%
7	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations: 15.37\% on SciERC (7 relations) and 18.46\% on DocRED (96 relations), comparable rates governed by \emph{constraint tightness} rather than ontology scale.
8	\textsc{OntoCoDec} reduces DR by 42--99\% across three model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) with no significant F1 cost.
9	For the primary model (Qwen3-8B): SciERC $+$0.13\,pp ($p{=}0.58$), DocRED $+$0.50\,pp ($p{<}0.001$); other models show comparable or larger gains (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
10	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1, mistral_docred
11	Residual DR correlates with the product of schema tightness and baseline violation rate: for calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), the dual-factor pattern explains nearly all variance ($R^2{=}0.99$, $n{=}4$), while Mistral's higher entity misclassification rate places it outside this regime (Appendix~\ref{app:dual-factor}).
12	Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning, DR reaches $\leq$\,0.09\% (SciERC) and 0.20\% (DocRED) with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp, confirming orthogonal composability.
13	Per-relation independent enforcement introduces a cross-field consistency (CF) vs.\ DR trade-off: CF violations arise (up to 12\% in zero-shot) as the cost of per-slot domain/range guarantees; fine-tuning resolves this tension (CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%).
14	\end{abstract}
15	
```

63	\clearpage
64	\section*{Limitations}
65	
66	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
67	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp), confirming that CF$\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
68	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
69	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
70	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on both SciERC and DocRED with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\%, R-F1 change within $\pm$0.6\,pp), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
71	The semantic layer's dynamic masking assumes each entity type maps to a unique first subword token, verified for both tokenizers on our two ontologies (6 entity types each), but ontologies with many types or multi-token type names would require prefix-tree masking, which we have not implemented.
72	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
73	All models tested are 7--8B-parameter class; scaling behavior to larger (70B+) or smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.
74	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
75	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
76	Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone (training-set frequency coverage), motivating complementary approaches such as global type consensus or post-hoc correction.
77	All main experiments use greedy decoding; preliminary evaluation of nucleus sampling and beam search (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}) suggests compatibility, but comprehensive evaluation across decoding configurations remains future work.
78	Validation with fine-tuned models, larger scales, and non-English ontologies remains to be explored.
79	
80	% === Ethics Statement ===
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}
5	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\cite{park2024grammar} ensure every token belongs to a grammar-valid continuation; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} adds backtracking.
6	These guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as ontology domain/range rules.
7	
8	\paragraph{Semantic Constrained Decoding for Code.}
9	Synchromesh~\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard}, MGD~\cite{mgd_neurips2023}, and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type and scope constraints in code generation; OntoCoDec migrates this paradigm to IE.
10	
11	\paragraph{Vocabulary and Type-Constrained Generation.}
12	\citet{roth_yih_2004} establish relational type compatibility as a hard constraint via ILP, though applied post-hoc.
13	GENRE~\cite{decao2021genre}, AdaKGC~\cite{adakgc_emnlp2023}, GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, and GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} constrain \emph{which tokens} can appear but not \emph{which type combinations} are valid per relation; GCD's per-position trie enforces vocabulary membership (equivalent to our grammar layer) but cannot capture the conditional dependency between head and tail types that our semantic layer provides.
14	DORE~\cite{vukovic2024dore} enforces ontological consistency via chain-of-thought output selection with confidence thresholds, a generate-then-select paradigm distinct from OntoCoDec's token-level logit masking during generation.
15	GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entity generation to a candidate trie but does not enforce relation-specific type constraints.
16	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints during decoding for joint entity-relation extraction, a hard-constraint approach like ours but with task-specific manually authored rules rather than ontology-compiled masks.
17	Training-based methods~\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie} and span-based models such as GLiNER~\cite{zaratiana2023gliner} encode IE structure via training or architecture but provide only \emph{soft} guidance.
18	Post-hoc approaches~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} validate outputs against ontologies after generation; OntoCoDec \emph{prevents} violations during decoding.
19	
20	\paragraph{Quality Impact of Constrained Decoding.}
21	CRANE~\cite{banerjee2025crane} and \citet{schall2025hiddencost} show grammar restrictions can degrade reasoning, but \citet{tam2024letmespeak} find extraction-style tasks are robust to (or benefit from) constraints.
22	BoostCD~\cite{sakota2025boostcd} combines constrained and unconstrained outputs via boosting for IE, complementing our decoding-time approach with a training-time strategy.
23	OntoCoDec confirms extraction robustness: violations decrease by 42--99\% with zero F1 degradation across three model families (extended survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}).
24

IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} adds backtracking.\nThese guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as ontology domain/range rules.\n\n\\paragraph{Semantic Constrained Decoding for Code.}\nSynchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard}, MGD~\\cite{mgd_neurips2023}, and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type and scope constraints in code generation; GCD's per-position trie enforces vocabulary membership (equivalent to our grammar layer) but cannot capture the conditional dependency between head and tail types that our semantic layer provides.\nDORE~\\cite{vukovic2024dore} enforces ontological consistency via chain-of-thought output selection with confidence thresholds, a generate-then-select paradigm distinct from OntoCoDec's token-level logit masking during generation.\nGenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entity generation to a candidate trie but does not enforce relation-specific type constraints.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints during decoding for joint entity-relation extraction, a hard-constraint approach like ours but with task-specific manually authored rules rather than ontology-compiled masks.\nTraining-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie} and span-based models such as GLiNER~\\cite{zaratiana2023gliner} encode IE structure via training or architecture but provide only \\emph{soft} guidance.\nPost-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} validate outputs against ontologies after generation; ```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly deployed for zero-shot information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	In a systematic study, we find violations are pervasive: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred}, driven by \emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology scale.
9	Relations with tight constraints exhibit far higher rates (\textsc{Compare}: 42.4\%) than loosely constrained ones (\textsc{Used-for}: 0.8\%).
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains, but at the cost of discarding up to 48\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it discards the majority of outputs, making it impractical.
15	OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation rather than discarding non-compliant outputs.
16	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot eliminate violations.
17	
18	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec} (\textbf{Onto}logy-\textbf{Co}mpiled \textbf{Dec}oding), a framework that compiles an IE ontology into two composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
19	The \emph{grammar layer} enforces JSON well-formedness and vocabulary validity via XGrammar.
20	The \emph{semantic layer} maintains a stateful entity registry during generation: as entities are decoded, the registry records type assignments; when relation arguments are generated, dynamic logit masking restricts output to types compatible with the relation's domain/range specification.
21	The layers compose via bitwise AND with an \emph{enabling dependency}: the grammar layer guarantees vocabulary membership, enabling the semantic layer to look up domain/range rules.
22	On SciERC, OntoCoDec reduces DR violations from 15.37\% to 0.93\% ($\downarrow$94\%) while remaining F1-neutral ($+$0.13\,pp).
23	
24	Our contributions:
25	\begin{enumerate}
26	    \item The first systematic study of ontological constraint violations in LLM-based IE (spanning per-relation tightness analysis, dual-factor characterization, and post-hoc baseline comparison), showing that violation rates are driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology size, and that prompt-level intervention is per-relation unreliable.
27	    \item OntoCoDec, a two-layer compiled constraint framework that is the first to enforce pair-based domain/range type constraints via compiled stateful logit masks during autoregressive IE.
28	    Unlike type-constrained decoding in code generation, where output types are statically determined by the grammar, IE requires stateful cross-field constraints because entity type validity depends on the co-occurring relation within each triple.
29	    \item Empirical validation on 3 models $\times$ 2 datasets: DR $\downarrow$76--99\% on SciERC and $\downarrow$42--90\% across three models on DocRED, with significant R-F1 gains on DocRED (Qwen $p{<}0.001$, Llama $p{=}0.013$), model-agnostic structural guarantees, composability with fine-tuning (DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\% at $<$0.6\,pp F1 cost), and characterization of the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off inherent to per-relation enforcement (resolved by fine-tuning to CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%).
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
```

Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
