1	# Document Length Breakdown: OntoCoDec Constraint Effectiveness
2	
3	## Summary
4	
5	OntoCoDec's violation reduction is **consistent across document lengths** (~74–84%), even though longer documents produce more total violations. Constraints are equally effective on complex (longer) documents as on simple (shorter) ones.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## 1. SciERC (Qwen3-8B, Zero-Shot)

39	| Long   | Constrained   | 30.0% | 7.3% |     5.9 |     4.1 |

66	| Short  | Constrained   |   0 |    0 |  495 | 138 |   633 |     69.6% |

68	| Medium | Constrained   |   0 |    5 |  544 | 180 |   729 |     77.0% |
69	| Long   | Unconstrained | 176 | 2209 | 1162 |   3 |  3550 |     98.7% |
70	| Long   | Constrained   |   0 |    0 |  731 | 200 |   931 |     80.7% |
71	
72	### 2.2 F1 (macro-avg over docs)
73	
74	| Bin    | Mode          | E-F1  | R-F1 | Avg Ent | Avg Rel |
75	|--------|---------------|-------|------|---------|---------|
76	| Short  | Unconstrained | 61.1% | 9.3% |    17.1 |    14.8 |
77	| Short  | Constrained   | 62.6% | 9.6% |    17.2 |    14.9 |
78	| Medium | Unconstrained | 59.3% | 7.9% |    20.3 |    17.6 |
79	| Medium | Constrained   | 60.7% | 8.5% |    20.3 |    17.5 |
80	| Long   | Unconstrained | 53.9% | 5.6% |    24.1 |    21.0 |
81	| Long   | Constrained   | 54.8% | 5.8% |    24.0 |    20.5 |

83	### 2.3 Constraint Effect
84	
85	| Bin    | Uncon Viols | Const Viols | Reduction | Reduction% |
86	|--------|-------------|-------------|-----------|------------|
87	| Short  |        2445 |         633 |      1812 |      74.1% |
88	| Medium |        2941 |         729 |      2212 |      75.2% |
89	| Long   |        3550 |         931 |      2619 |      73.8% |
90	
91	---
92	
93	## 3. Key Findings
94	
95	1. **Consistent reduction across lengths**: Violation reduction is 80–84% for SciERC and 74–75% for DocRED, with no significant degradation on longer documents.
96	
97	2. **Longer docs produce more violations**: In DocRED, long docs average 10.8 violations/doc unconstrained vs 7.3 for short docs. OntoCoDec scales proportionally.
98	
99	3. **ET and RV fully eliminated**: Grammar-constrained decoding eliminates 100% of entity-type and relation-vocabulary violations across all length bins (both datasets).
100	
101	4. **Residual DR violations in DocRED**: Domain-range violations remain in constrained mode (495–731 per bin). This is consistent with the known limitation that DocRED's 96 relation types create a larger semantic constraint space.
102	
103	5. **CF violations trade-off**: In SciERC, constrained mode introduces new CF violations (0→5/15/20 across bins) while eliminating ET/RV. The grammar forces valid types, but sometimes assigns different valid types to the same entity across relations.
104	
105	6. **F1 preserved or improved**: Constrained decoding does not harm extraction quality. DocRED shows slight E-F1 improvement (+0.9–1.5pp) and R-F1 improvement (+0.2–0.6pp) across all bins. SciERC is essentially unchanged.
106	
107	7. **Long-doc effectiveness**: The most important finding: long documents (where violations are most prevalent) benefit equally from OntoCoDec. The constraint framework does not degrade with document complexity.
108

- ET: 0 ✓ (report: 0+0+0=0) ✓
- RV: 0 ✓ (report: 0+0+0=0) ✓

- CF: 0.099 → checking if this is per-triple or another metric

. The beam search shows CF at 4.12% with 71 violations, which gives a denominator of ~1723 (matching total_triples). For greedy decoding at 9.9%, I need to figure out what denominator produces the reported count of 40.

I'm noticing a significant gap between the violation_checker's count of ~171 CF events and the doc_length report's count of 40. The worker might be counting unique entities with type conflicts rather than individual affected triples — so if one entity has inconsistent types across multiple relations, that's one unique CF event in the worker's logic but multiple violations in the checker's logic. For the document length analysis, this distinction matters depending on what we're actually trying to measure.

Now I'm moving through the DocRED Constrained dataset to verify the violation counts. The ET violations check out perfectly at zero, RV is close at 5 versus the expected 5.2, but DR shows a larger discrepancy with the report showing 495+544 when I'd expect around 1771 based on the error rate.

For the CF violations, I'm finding that treating it as per-entity calculations (0.0257 × 20187) gives me 518.8, which matches the reported 518 exactly—so that's the right interpretation.

Moving into the DocRED Unconstrained dataset, the ET violations at 500 and RV violations at 5189 both align perfectly with the per-entity and per-triple calculations respectively. Now I'm checking the DR violations against the 0.1846 error rate.

For the CF violations here, I'm getting inconsistent results depending on whether I use the entity count or triple count as the denominator—0.001 × 20171 gives 20.2 while 0.001 × 17497 gives 17.5, and the reported value is 17. Let me reconsider which denominator actually applies to the constrained versus unconstrained versions. I'm realizing the DocRED unconstrained rate of 0.001 is actually a rounded value—17 violations out of 20,171 entities equals 0.000843, which rounds to 0.001 when expressed as a percentage. So the denominator is consistently entities across both constrained and unconstrained cases, though there's a small discrepancy of 3 violations between the calculated and reported numbers that I'm still trying to account for.

But SciERC is a different story entirely—the constrained CF rate of 0.099 (9.9%) doesn't match the report's 40 violations no matter what denominator I try. If I work backwards from 40 violations and the 9.9% rate, the implied denominator would be 404, which doesn't correspond to any metric in the SciERC dataset.

I think the key difference is that the doc_length worker counts unique entities with conflicting types, while the checker counts total type violations across all relation slots. For SciERC, if 40 entities each appear in roughly 4.3 relations on average, that gives about 171 violations—matching the checker's count. DocRED follows a similar pattern but with fewer relations per entity on average, so the unique entity count aligns with the violation count. The doc_length breakdown is useful data but has one clear issue: SciERC CF absolute counts (40 total) are inconsistent with the known global CF rate (9.9% of 1729 triples ≈ 171), likely because the worker counts unique entities with CF issues rather than affected triple slots without documenting this distinction.

The main conclusion about consistent reduction across document lengths holds up though—DR verification matches the registry exactly for both datasets, ET/RV checks out perfectly, and the relative patterns remain valid regardless of how CF is counted. The DR-specific decline on longer DocRED documents is worth noting but doesn't undermine the overall finding.
